\mathbb {E} [ (Q - \mathbb {E} Q) ^ {2 p} ] \leq \mathcal {C} n ^ {- 1. 5} \max _ {i \in [ n ]} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {N} (0, \Pi_ {i i})} {\max} \mathbb {E} \left(\phi_ {i} (x) - \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {2 p}
$$

for some constant  $\mathcal{C}$  depending on  $p$  and  $R$ , but not on  $n$  or the functions  $\phi_i$ . If in addition, each  $\phi_i$  has finite centered moments of order  $2pL$  for some  $L > 1$ , then

$$
\mathbb {E} [ (Q - \mathbb {E} Q) ^ {2 p} ] \leq \mathcal {C} n ^ {- 1. 5 + 1 / L} \sqrt [ L ]{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {N} (0 , \Pi_ {i i})} \left(\phi_ {i} (x) - \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {2 p L}}.
$$

Proof. See Yang [64].

![](images/703f21669083685850d37d1211c96b7b18f163c70149d99efae9e30e196468df.jpg)

# G Proof of Main Theorem

In this section, we will give the proof for Theorem A.6 which is equivalent to Theorem 7.2. We reproduce the statement below

Theorem A.6 (BP-like NETSOR  $\top$  Master Theorem). Fix any BP-like NETSOR  $\top$  program satisfying Setup A.5 and with all nonlinearities polynomially-bounded. If  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{M}$  are all of the  $G$ -vars in the entire program, including all input  $G$ -vars, then for any polynomially-bounded  $\psi :\mathbb{R}^M\to \mathbb{R}$ , as  $n\to \infty$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \ldots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {M}) \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z) = \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z ^ {g ^ {1}}, \ldots , Z ^ {g ^ {M}}),
$$

where  $\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}$  means almost sure convergence,  $Z = (Z^{g^1},\dots,Z^{g^M})\in \mathbb{R}^M$ , and  $\mu = \{\mu(g^i)\}_{i=1}^M \in \mathbb{R}^M$  and  $\Sigma = \{\Sigma(g^i,g^j)\}_{i,j=1}^M \in \mathbb{R}^{M \times M}$  are given in Eq. (18). See Fig. 1 for an illustration.

Comparison against NETSOR Master Theorem [62] We will follow the general outline of the inductive proof of NETSOR Master Theorem in Yang [62]. Here, the correlation of a matrix with its transpose (Remark G.1) causes additional difficulty in proving rank stability and zero stability properties (Appendix G.3) as well as the induction hypothesis  $(\mathrm{Moments}(m))$ . The main sections dealing with these difficulties are Appendix G.2 which describes the setup for the induction, Appendix G.4.2 which proves part of rank and zero stability properties, and Appendices G.5.2 and G.5.3 which prove parts of the inductive step using the law of large numbers for weakly correlated random variables Theorem F.8.

A Bit of Notation and Terminology Note that, for each  $n$ , the randomness of our program specified by Theorem A.6 comes from the sampling of the input variables. Let  $\mathcal{U}$  be the product space obtained from multiplying together the corresponding probability space for each  $n$ . Each sample from this product probability space thus corresponds to a sequence  $\{S(n)\}_{n}$  of instantiations of input variables. Below, when we say "almost surely" (often abbreviated "a.s.), we mean "almost surely over the probability of  $\mathcal{U}$ ". We will also often make statements of the form

$$
a l m o s t \quad a l m e s u r e l y (o r, a. s.), f o r a l l \quad \text {l a r g e} n, \quad \mathcal {A} (n) i s t r u e
$$

where  $\mathcal{A}(n)$  is a claim parametrized by  $n$ . This means that for all but a  $\mathcal{U}$ -probability-zero set of sequences  $\{S(n)\}_{n}$  of input variable instantiations,  $\mathcal{A}(n)$  is true for large enough  $n$ . Note that the order of the qualifiers is very important here.

We induct, but on what? A natural way of going about proving Theorem A.6 is by inducting on the number of variables in a program. It turns out this is not enough to prove our claim in its full generality, and it would be more fruitful to perform a simultaneous induction on our claim (Moments) along with another statement, parametrized by  $m$ ,

Moments(m) For any polynomially-bounded  $\psi : \mathbb{R}^m \to \mathbb{R}$ , as  $n \to \infty$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m}\right) \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z).
$$

CoreSet(m) There exists a "core set"  $\mathcal{M} \subseteq [m]$  such that,

Basis  $(m)$  almost surely, for large enough  $n$ , for every  $i \in [m]$ , there exist unique constants (not depending on  $n$ )  $\{a_j\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}$  such that  $g^i = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{M}} a_j g^j$ . Note the uniqueness implies that  $\{g^i\}_{i \in \mathcal{M}}$  is linearly independent.

NullAvoid  $(m)$  for every triangular array of Lesbegue measure zero sets  $\{A_{n\alpha} \in \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}, \alpha \in [n]}$ , almost surely for all large enough  $n$ , for all  $\alpha \in [n]$ , we have

$$
\left\{g _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \right\} _ {i \in \mathcal {M}} \notin A _ {n \alpha}.
$$

In other words, the values  $\{g_{\alpha}^{i}\}_{\alpha \in \mathcal{M}}$  of the core set "avoid" Lebesgue measure zero sets asymptotically. Intuitively, this says that the distribution of these values are not singular. (Note the LHS depends on  $n$  although we are suppressing it notationally)

Let us explain in brief why we need to consider CoreSet satisfying Basis and NullAvoid.

- Basis reduces the consideration of Moments to only the core set G-vars, since every other G-var is asymptotically a linear combination of them.  
- When we apply the Gaussian conditioning technique Proposition F.3, we need to reason about the pseudo-inverse  $\Lambda^{+}$  of some submatrix  $\Lambda$  of a covariance matrix. Each entry of  $\Lambda$  is of the form  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\phi_{i}(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1})\phi_{j}(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1})$  for a collection of polynomially bounded scalar functions  $\{\phi_i\}_i$ . This  $\Lambda$  will be a random variable which converges a.s. to a deterministic limit  $\hat{\Lambda}$  as  $n\to \infty$ . It should be generically true that  $\Lambda^{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}$  as well, which is essential to make the Gaussian conditioning argument go through. But in general, this is guaranteed only if  $\Lambda$  's rank doesn't drop suddenly in the  $n\to \infty$  limit. We thus need to guard against the possibility that  $g^1,\dots,g^m$ , in the limit, suddenly concentrate on a small set on which  $\{\phi_i(g^1,\dots,g^m)\}_{i}$  are linearly dependent. This is where NullAvoid comes in. It tells us that  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m}$  will avoid any such small set asymptotically, so that indeed the rank of  $\Lambda$  will not drop in the limit.

Proof organization We will show that Moments and CoreSet are true for input variables, as the base case, and

$$
\operatorname {M o m e n t s} (m - 1) \text {a n d C o r e S e t} (m - 1) \Longrightarrow \operatorname {M o m e n t s} (m) \text {a n d C o r e S e t} (m)
$$

as the inductive step. By induction, we obtain Moments  $(M)$ , which is Theorem A.6.

The base cases are easy and we will dispatch with them immediately after this in Appendix G.1, but the inductive step is much more complicated, and we will need to set up notation in Appendix G.2. During this setup, we prove some basic limit theorems using the induction hypothesis. However, the full generality of these claims requires some consequences of CoreSet, which we call "rank stability" and "zero stability." These notions are introduced and proved in Appendix G.3.

We would then finally be able to handle the inductive steps at this point. We first prove

$$
\text {M o m e n t s} (m - 1) \text {a n d C o r e S e t} (m - 1) \Longrightarrow \text {C o r e S e t} (m)
$$

in Appendix G.4 because it is easier. Then we prove

$$
\text {M o m e n t s} (m - 1) \text {a n d C o r e S e t} (m - 1) \Longrightarrow \text {M o m e n t s} (m)
$$

in Appendix G.5.

# G.1 Base Cases: Moments and CoreSet for Input Variables

Base case: Moments(input vars) Suppose the input variables are  $x^{1}, \ldots, x^{k} : \mathsf{G}(n)$  (so that  $\mu \in \mathbb{R}^k$ ,  $\Sigma \in \mathbb{R}^{k \times k}$ ). We need to show that for any polynomially-bounded function  $\psi : \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi (x _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \ldots , x _ {\alpha} ^ {k}) \stackrel {{\mathrm {a . s .}}} {{\longrightarrow}} \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z),
$$

where  $\psi$  on the RHS ignores all coordinates corresponding to non-input G-vars. Since  $\mu$  and  $\Sigma$  restricted to input variables are just  $\mu$  and  $\Sigma$  (see Eq. (18)), the RHS expectation is just

$$
\underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z) = \underset {Z ^ {i n} \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z ^ {i n})
$$

and the almost sure convergence we desire is just a result of the law of large numbers.

Base Case: CoreSet(input vars) Let  $x^1, \ldots, x^k$  be the input G-vars as above. Pick the core set  $\mathcal{M}$  to be any subset of  $[k]$  such that  $\text{rank } \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}} = \text{rank } \Sigma$ . Then it's straightforward to verify Basis and NullAvoid.

# G.2 Inductive Case: Setup

We now assume Moments  $(m - 1)$  and CoreSet  $(m - 1)$  and want to reason about  $g^{m}$  to show Moments  $(m)$  and CoreSet  $(m)$ . Suppose

$$
g ^ {m} := A h \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad A: \mathrm {A} (n, n) \text {a n d} h: \mathrm {H} (n) \text {w a s i n t r o d u c e d b y} h := \phi \left(g ^ {1}, \dots , g ^ {m - 1}\right)
$$

(WLOG padding input slots if necessary; if  $h = g^i$  is a G-var, then just let  $\phi$  be the projection to the  $i$ th coordinate). For brevity, we will just write  $g = g^m$ . Consider all previous instances where  $A$  or  $A^\top$  is used:

$$
\hat {g} ^ {i} := A \hat {h} ^ {i}, i = 1, \dots , r, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \check {g} ^ {j} := A ^ {\top} \check {h} ^ {j}, j = 1, \dots , s.
$$

Define

$$
\hat {G} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} [ \hat {g} ^ {1} | \dots | \hat {g} ^ {r} ] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times r}, \check {G} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} [ \check {g} ^ {1} | \dots | \check {g} ^ {s} ] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times s}, \hat {H} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} [ \hat {h} ^ {1} | \dots | \hat {h} ^ {r} ], \check {H} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} [ \check {h} ^ {1} | \dots | \check {h} ^ {s} ]. \tag {40}
$$

We will also use  $\hat{G}$  to denote the set of G-vars  $\{\hat{g}^1,\dots ,\hat{g}^r\}$  when we later write expressions like  $\Sigma (\hat{G},\hat{G})$ . Let  $\mathcal{B}$  be the  $\sigma$ -algebra spanned by all previous G-vars  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m - 1}$  (and hence also all previous H-vars). Conditioning on  $\mathcal{B}$ ,  $A$  is constrained by  $\hat{G} = A\hat{H}$ ,  $\check{G} = A^{\top}\check{H}$ , and we have by Lemma F.6,

$$
g \stackrel {{\mathrm {d}}} {{=}} _ {\mathcal {B}} \left(E + \Pi_ {\tilde {H}} ^ {\perp} \tilde {A} \Pi_ {\hat {H}} ^ {\perp}\right) h
$$

where

$$
\begin{array}{l} E = \hat {G} \hat {H} ^ {+} + \tilde {H} ^ {+ \top} \check {G} ^ {\top} - \check {H} ^ {+ \top} \check {G} ^ {\top} \hat {H} \hat {H} ^ {+} \\ = \hat {G} (\hat {H} ^ {\top} \hat {H}) ^ {+} \hat {H} ^ {\top} + \check {H} (\check {H} ^ {\top} \check {H}) ^ {+} \check {G} ^ {\top} - \check {H} (\check {H} ^ {\top} \check {H}) ^ {+} \check {G} ^ {\top} \hat {H} (\hat {H} ^ {\top} \hat {H}) ^ {+} \hat {H} ^ {\top}, \tag {41} \\ \end{array}
$$

$\tilde{A}$  is an independent copy of  $A$  and  $\Pi_{\hat{H}} = \hat{H}\hat{H}^{+} = \hat{H} (\hat{H}^{\top}\hat{H})^{+}\hat{H}^{\top}$  is the projection to the column space of  $\hat{H}$  (likewise for  $\Pi_{\check{H}}$ ).

Remark G.1. Note if Trsp is not allowed (as in NETSOR Master Theorem [62]), then this would simplify a lot to  $g \stackrel{\mathrm{d}}{=}_{\mathcal{B}} (\hat{G} \hat{H}^{+} + \tilde{A} \Pi_{\hat{H}}^{\perp}) h$ . In particular, compared to the NETSOR Master Theorem, we cannot straightforwardly think of  $g$  as having iid Gaussian coordinates because of the projection  $\Pi_{\hat{H}}^{\perp}$  in front of  $\tilde{A}$ . Most of the added complexity of this proof of NETSOR T Master Theorem comes from this fact.

Remark G.2. On the other hand, with the BP-like assumption,  $E$  is roughly equal to  $\hat{G}\hat{H}^{+}$ ; see Lemma G.5. However, this is very far from true when we don't assume the program is BP-like.

If we define

$$
\omega \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} E h, \quad \sigma \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \sigma_ {A} \sqrt {\left\| \Pi_ {\hat {H}} ^ {\perp} h \right\| ^ {2} / n} \tag {42}
$$

then

$$
g \stackrel {\mathrm {d}} {=} _ {\mathcal {B}} \omega + \sigma \Pi_ {\check {H}} ^ {\perp} y, \text {w i t h} y \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I _ {n}) \tag {43}
$$

For brevity, we will define the following matrices and vectors of fixed dimension

$$
\hat {\Lambda} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \hat {H} ^ {\top} \hat {H} / n \in \mathbb {R} ^ {r \times r} \quad \check {\Lambda} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \check {H} ^ {\top} \check {H} / n \in \mathbb {R} ^ {s \times s} \quad \Gamma \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \check {G} ^ {\top} \hat {H} / n \in \mathbb {R} ^ {s \times r} \tag {44}
$$

$$
\hat {\eta} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \hat {H} ^ {\top} h / n \in \mathbb {R} ^ {r} \qquad \check {\eta} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \check {G} ^ {\top} h / n \in \mathbb {R} ^ {s}.
$$

Suppose  $\hat{h}^i$  was introduced by  $\hat{h}^i \coloneqq \hat{\phi}^i(g^1, \ldots, g^M)$ , and  $\check{h}^j$  was introduced by  $\check{h}^j \coloneqq \check{\phi}^j(g^1, \ldots, g^M)$ , where  $\hat{\phi}^i$  and  $\check{\phi}^j$  depend at most on  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$ . By induction hypothesis Moments $(m-1), \hat{\Lambda}, \check{\Lambda}, \Gamma, \hat{\eta}, \check{\eta}$  all converge a.s. to corresponding limit values  $\stackrel{\circ}{\Lambda}, \stackrel{\circ}{\Lambda}, \stackrel{\circ}{\eta}$ , since their entries are moments of  $Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}$ :

$$
\hat {\Lambda} _ {i j} \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} \stackrel {\circ} {\hat {\Lambda}} _ {i j} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \mathbb {E} \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (Z) \hat {\phi} ^ {j} (Z) = (\sigma_ {A}) ^ {- 2} \Sigma (\hat {g} ^ {i}, \hat {g} ^ {j})
$$

$$
\check {\Lambda} _ {i j} \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} \stackrel {\circ} {\check {\Lambda}} _ {i j} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \mathbb {E} \check {\phi} ^ {i} (Z) \check {\phi} ^ {j} (Z) = (\sigma_ {A}) ^ {- 2} \Sigma (\check {g} ^ {i}, \check {g} ^ {j})
$$

$$
\hat {\eta} _ {i} \stackrel {\mathrm {a . s .}} {\longrightarrow} \stackrel {\circ} {\hat {\eta} _ {i}} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \mathbb {E} \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (Z) \phi (Z) = (\sigma_ {A}) ^ {- 2} \Sigma (\hat {g} ^ {i}, g)
$$

and  $\Gamma \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0, \check{\eta} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  because, by the BP-like assumption,  $\check{G}$  is odd in some input G-vars  $v^1, \ldots, v^k$  independent from all other input G-vars, so that the limiting expectation is 0.

It turns out that, as a consequence of Lemma G.6 below, a.s. for all large enough  $n$ ,  $\mathrm{rank}\hat{\Lambda} = \mathrm{rank}\mathring{\Lambda}$  and  $\mathrm{rank}\check{\Lambda} = \mathrm{rank}\mathring{\Lambda}$ . Therefore, as pseudoinverse is continuous on matrices of fixed rank, we get the following proposition

Proposition G.3.  $\hat{\Lambda}^{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}\stackrel {\circ}{\Lambda}^{+}$  and  $\check{\Lambda}^{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}\check{\Lambda}^{+}$ .

Using this proposition, we compute the limits of the conditional mean  $\omega$  and variance  $\sigma^2$ .

Lemma G.4.  $\sigma^2\stackrel {\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\mathring{\sigma}^2\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\Sigma (g,g) - \Sigma (g,\hat{G})\Sigma (\hat{G},\hat{G})^{+}\Sigma (\hat{G},g)$

Proof. Note that

$$
\sigma^ {2} = \frac {\sigma_ {A} ^ {2}}{n} (h ^ {\top} h - h ^ {\top} \Pi_ {\hat {H}} h) = \frac {\sigma_ {A} ^ {2}}{n} (h ^ {\top} h - h ^ {\top} \hat {H} (\hat {H} ^ {\top} \hat {H}) ^ {+} \hat {H} ^ {\top} h) = \frac {\sigma_ {A} ^ {2}}{n} (h ^ {\top} h - \hat {\eta} ^ {\top} \hat {\Lambda} ^ {+} \hat {\eta}).
$$

Because  $\phi$  is polynomially-bounded, so is  $\phi(z)^2$  as well. By induction hypothesis,

$$
\frac {1}{n} h ^ {\top} h = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \phi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \ldots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) ^ {2} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \phi (Z) ^ {2} = \sigma_ {A} ^ {- 2} \Sigma (g, g).
$$

Likewise,  $\hat{\eta} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \stackrel{\circ}{\hat{\eta}}$  and  $\hat{\Lambda} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \stackrel{\circ}{\hat{\Lambda}}$ . By Proposition G.3,  $\hat{\Lambda}^{+} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \stackrel{\circ}{\hat{\Lambda}^{+}}$ . Combining all of these limits together yields the desired claim.

Lemma G.5. Let  $v \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \hat{\Lambda}^{+}\hat{\eta}$ , so that  $v \stackrel{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow} \bar{v} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{\longrightarrow} \stackrel{\stackrel{\circ}{\Lambda}^{+}\stackrel{\circ}{\eta}}{\longrightarrow}$ . Then for some vector  $\hat{\varepsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^r$ ,  $\check{\varepsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^s$  that go to 0 a.s. with  $n$ ,  $\omega = Eh = \hat{G}(\hat{v} + \hat{\varepsilon}) + \check{H}\check{\varepsilon}$

Proof. Using Eqs. (41) and (44), we can re-express  $\omega$  as

$$
\omega = \hat {G} \hat {\Lambda} ^ {+} \hat {\eta} + \tilde {H} \tilde {\Lambda} ^ {+} \hat {\eta} - \tilde {H} \tilde {\Lambda} ^ {+} \Gamma \hat {\Lambda} ^ {+} \hat {\eta}.
$$

Because  $\Gamma \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0, \check{\eta} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  as discussed above, we can set  $\check{\varepsilon} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \check{\Lambda}^{+} \check{\eta} - \check{\Lambda}^{+} \Gamma \hat{\Lambda}^{+} \hat{\eta}$ , so that  $\check{\varepsilon} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$ . In addition, by Proposition G.3,  $\hat{\Lambda}^{+} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \stackrel{\circ}{\hat{\Lambda}^{+}}$ , so that setting  $\hat{\varepsilon} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} v - \stackrel{\circ}{v}$ , we get  $\hat{\varepsilon} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$ .

Altogether, we have

$$
\omega = \hat {G} (\mathring {v} + \hat {\varepsilon}) + \check {H} \check {\varepsilon}
$$

as desired.

![](images/3e53f4dab6920d179964fa59bb32997028011991cdc785161ac3d257eb1abad8.jpg)

# G.3 Rank Stability and Zero Stability

In this section, we prove the following consequence of CoreSet  $(m - 1)$  and Moments  $(m - 1)$ .

Lemma G.6 (Rank Stability). For any collection of polynomially-bounded functions  $\{\psi_j : \mathbb{R}^{m-1} \to \mathbb{R}\}_{j=1}^l$ , let  $K \in \mathbb{R}^{l \times l}$  be the random matrix (depending on  $n$ ) defined by

$$
K _ {i j} = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi_ {i} \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) \psi_ {j} \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right).
$$

By Moments  $(m - 1)$

$$
K \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} \mathring {K}
$$

for some matrix  $\hat{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{l \times l}$ .

1. Then, almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\ker K = \ker \stackrel {\circ} {K}, \quad \operatorname {i m} K = \operatorname {i m} \stackrel {\circ} {K}, \quad a n d \quad \operatorname {r a n k} K = \operatorname {r a n k} \stackrel {\circ} {K}.
$$

Here  $\ker$  denotes null space and  $\operatorname{im}$  denotes image space.

2. Suppose  $I \subseteq [l]$  is any subset such that  $\tilde{K}|_I$ , the restriction of  $\tilde{K}$  to rows and columns corresponding to  $I$ , satisfies

$$
| I | = \operatorname {r a n k} \mathring {K} | _ {I} = \operatorname {r a n k} \mathring {K}.
$$

There are unique coefficients  $\{F_{ij}\}_{i\in [l],j\in I}$  that express each row of  $\hat{K}$  as linear combinations of rows corresponding to  $I$ :

$$
\forall i \in [ l ], \quad \mathring {K} _ {i} = \sum_ {j \in I} F _ {i j} \mathring {K} _ {j}.
$$

Then, a.s. for all large  $n$ , for all  $\alpha \in [n]$

$$
\psi_ {i} (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) = \sum_ {j \in I} F _ {i j} \psi_ {j} (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}).
$$

This will be primarily a corollary of the following Lemma G.7.

Lemma G.7 (Zero Stability). If  $\psi : \mathbb{R}^{m-1} \to \mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}$  is a nonnegative function such that

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} 0
$$

then, almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) = 0
$$

for all  $\alpha \in [n]$

We give the proof of Lemma G.6 now, assuming Lemma G.7.

Proof. Let  $v \in \mathbb{R}^l$  be in the null space of  $\hat{K}$ , i.e.  $v^\top \hat{K}v = 0$ . Then we also have  $v^\top Kv \xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}} v^\top \hat{K}v = 0$ . But

$$
v ^ {\top} K v = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \Psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right), \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad \Psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} v _ {i} \psi_ {i} \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right)\right) ^ {2}
$$

and  $\Psi$  is a nonnegative function. By Lemma G.7, we have that: almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\Psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) = 0 \quad \text {f o r a l l} \alpha \in [ n ] \quad \Longrightarrow v ^ {\top} K v = 0
$$

Claim 1. If we apply this argument to a basis  $\{v^1,\dots ,v^t\}$  of  $\ker \mathring{K}$ , then we get,

a.s. for all large  $n$ ,  $\ker \tilde{K} \subseteq \ker K$ ,

so that

a.s. for all large  $n$ ,  $\operatorname{rank} \tilde{K} \geq \operatorname{rank} K$ .

Because the rank function is lower semicontinuous (i.e. the rank can drop suddenly, but cannot increase suddenly), and  $K \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \mathring{K}$ , we also have

a.s. for all large  $n$ ,  $\operatorname{rank} \hat{K} \leq \operatorname{rank} K$ .

Combined with the above, this gives the desired result on rank. The equality of null space then follows from the equality of rank, and the equality of image space follows immediately, as the image space is the orthogonal complement of the null space.

Claim 2. If we apply the above argument to each  $v^i$  defined by inner product as

$$
\forall x \in \mathbb {R} ^ {l}, \quad x ^ {\top} v ^ {i} \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} x _ {i} - \sum_ {j \in I} F _ {i j} x _ {j},
$$

(note that only for  $i \notin I$  is  $v^i$  nonzero), then we have, a.s. for large  $n$ ,  $v^{i\top}Kv^i = 0$ , or

$$
\psi_ {i} \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) = \sum_ {j \in I} F _ {i j} \psi_ {j} \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right).
$$

![](images/a704323f3d84e6dff05a712adbd739208e5494697b03a40c0cb7b1c40b3f4c53.jpg)

In the rest of this section, we prove Lemma G.7. It helps to first show that the linear relations given in Basis carries over to the  $n\to \infty$  limit.

Proposition G.8. Let  $\Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}$  be the submatrix of  $\Sigma$  with rows and columns corresponding to  $\{g^i : i \in \mathcal{M}\}$ . Then  $\operatorname{rank} \Sigma = \operatorname{rank} \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}} = |\mathcal{M}|$ . Furthermore, if  $Z = (Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu|_{m-1}, \Sigma|_{m-1})$ , where  $\mu|_{m-1}, \Sigma|_{m-1}$  are the restrictions of  $\mu, \Sigma$  to  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$ , then for each  $i$ ,

$$
Z ^ {i} \stackrel {\mathrm {d}} {=} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {M}} a _ {j} Z ^ {j}
$$

where  $\{a_j\}_{j\in \mathcal{M}}$  are the coefficients corresponding to  $g^i$  given in Basis.

Proof. By Basis property, each  $g^i, i \in \mathcal{M}$ , has a set of unique constants  $\{a_j\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}$  (independent of  $n$ ) such that, almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
g ^ {i} = \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {M}} a _ {j} g ^ {j}.
$$

Let  $\psi(x^1, \ldots, x^{m-1}) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} (x^i - \sum_{j \in \mathcal{M}} a_j x^j)^2$ . Then by Basis(m-1) and Moments(m-1),

$$
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\psi (g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1}) \xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}} \underset {Z\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu |_{m - 1},\Sigma |_{m - 1})}{\mathbb{E}}\psi (Z) = 0.
$$

where  $\mu|_{m-1}, \Sigma|_{m-1}$  are the restrictions of  $\mu, \Sigma$  to  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$ . This implies that for  $Z = (Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu|_{m-1}, \Sigma|_{m-1})$ ,

$$
Z ^ {i} \stackrel {\mathrm {d}} {=} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {M}} a _ {j} Z ^ {j}.
$$

Repeating this argument for all  $i \in [m - 1]$  implies that  $\{Z^j\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}$  is a "spanning set" of  $Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}$ . Furthermore, by the uniqueness of the coefficients, we also have that  $\{Z^j\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}$  is linearly independent as well. This then implies the rank consequence we want.

Now we show Lemma G.7.

Proof of Lemma G.7. By Moments  $(m - 1)$

$$
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\psi (g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1})\to \underset {Z\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu |_{m - 1},\Sigma |_{m - 1})}{\mathbb{E}}\psi (Z).
$$

By Proposition G.8, if  $Z \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu|_{m-1}, \Sigma|_{m-1})$  and  $Z|_{\mathcal{M}}$  is the part of  $Z$  corresponding to  $\mathcal{M}$ , then

$Z|_{\mathcal{M}}$  has density. The law of  $Z|_{\mathcal{M}}$  (namely  $\mathcal{N}(\mu |_{\mathcal{M}},\Sigma |_{\mathcal{M}})$ , where  $\mu |_{\mathcal{M}},\Sigma |_{\mathcal{M}}$  are the restriction of  $\mu$  and  $\Sigma$  to  $\mathcal{M}$ ) is absolutely continuous against the Lebesgue measure of  $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}$  and vice versa, so that a set of Lebesgue measure zero is measure zero under  $\mathcal{N}(\mu |_{\mathcal{M}},\Sigma |_{\mathcal{M}})$ , and vice versa; and

$Z|_{\mathcal{M}}$  is basis of  $Z$ . Basis yields a linear function  $\lambda$  such that  $\lambda(\{g_{\alpha}^{j}\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}) = \{g_{\alpha}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}$  for all  $\alpha$ , almost surely asymptotically, and  $\lambda(Z|_{\mathcal{M}}) \stackrel{\mathrm{d}}{=} Z$ , so that

$$
\operatorname *{\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu |_{m - 1},\Sigma |_{m - 1})}\psi (Z) = \operatorname *{\mathbb{E}}_{Z^{\prime}\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu |_{\mathcal{M}},\Sigma |_{\mathcal{M}})}\psi \circ \lambda (Z^{\prime}).
$$

This expectation is 0 by our premise.

Because  $\psi$ , and thus  $\psi \circ \lambda$ , is a nonnegative function, the nullity of the expectation implies that, other than a set  $U$  of  $\mathcal{N}(\mu|_{\mathcal{M}}, \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}})$ -measure zero,  $\psi \circ \lambda$  is 0. This set  $U$  also has Lebesgue measure zero as  $Z|_{\mathcal{M}}$  has density, by our reasoning above.

If in NullAvoid, we set  $A_{n\alpha} = U$  for all  $n$  and all  $\alpha \in [n]$ , then we get that: almost surely, for all large enough  $n$ , for all  $\alpha \in [n]$ ,

$$
\{g _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \} _ {i \in \mathcal {M}} \notin U \iff \psi \circ \lambda (\{g _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \} _ {i \in \mathcal {M}}) = 0 \iff \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \ldots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) = 0,
$$

as desired.

![](images/8492c6797c4c17258ed6660a4f7cf2a860a6cc79e26a6e3b33e6dcae37c9cdfd.jpg)

# G.4 Inductive Step: CoreSet(m)

In this section, we show

$$
\text {M o m e n t s} (m - 1) \text {a n d C o r e S e t} (m - 1) \Longrightarrow \text {C o r e S e t} (m).
$$

More explicitly, we need to think about whether to add  $m$  to the core set  $\mathcal{M}$  of  $[m - 1]$  in order to maintain the Basis and NullAvoid properties.

We proceed by casework on whether  $\delta = 0$

# G.4.1 If  $\mathring{\sigma} = 0$

We will show that the core set properties are maintained if we don't add  $m$  to the core set.

Consider the space  $\mathcal{L} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} L^2(\mathcal{N}(\mu|_{\mathcal{M}}, \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}))$  of square-integrable real functions against the measure  $\mathcal{N}(\mu|_{\mathcal{M}}, \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}})$  defined on  $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}$ . Let  $\langle \phi, \psi \rangle = \mathbb{E}_{Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu|_{\mathcal{M}}, \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}})} \phi(Y) \psi(Y)$  be the inner product of this space. Just like in a finite-dimensional inner product space, given a finite collection of functions  $S = \{\psi^i\}_{i=1}^k$ , the orthogonal projection operator  $\Pi_S$  to the span of  $S$  (inside  $\mathcal{L}$ ) is given by

$$
\Pi_ {S} \phi = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} a _ {i} \psi^ {i},
$$

for any  $\phi \in \mathcal{L}$ , where

$$
a = \Lambda^ {+} b \in \mathbb {R} ^ {k},
$$

$$
b _ {j} = \langle \psi^ {j}, \phi \rangle , b \in \mathbb {R} ^ {k},
$$

$$
\Lambda_ {i j} = \langle \psi^ {i}, \psi^ {j} \rangle , \Lambda \in \mathbb {R} ^ {k \times k}.
$$

Recall that  $g = Ah$  where  $h$  was introduced by  $h \coloneqq \phi(g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1})$ , for some polynomially-bounded  $\phi$ , and likewise  $\hat{g}^i = A\hat{h}^i$  where  $\hat{h}^i = \hat{\phi}^i(g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1})$ , for each  $i \in [r]$ . By Basis, we know that, a.s. for large enough  $n$ , each of  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$  is a (unique, constant-in- $n$ ) linear combination of  $\{g^j\}_{j \in \mathcal{M}}$ . Therefore, we can express

$$
h = \underline {{\phi}} (\{g ^ {j} \} _ {j \in \mathcal {M}}), \quad \text {a n d} \quad \forall i \in [ r ], \hat {h} ^ {i} = \underline {{\hat {\phi}}} ^ {i} (\{g ^ {j} \} _ {j \in \mathcal {M}})
$$

for some functions  $\underline{\phi},\underline{\hat{\phi}}^i\in \mathcal{L}$ . For convenience, set  $S\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\{\underline{\hat{\phi}}^i\}_i$

One can see then, as in the proof of Lemma G.4,

$$
\mathring {\sigma} ^ {2} = \sigma_ {A} ^ {2} (\mathbb {E} \phi (Z) ^ {2} - \mathring {\hat {\eta}} ^ {\top} \mathring {\Lambda} ^ {+} \mathring {\hat {\eta}}) = \sigma_ {A} ^ {2} (\langle \underline {{\phi}}, \underline {{\phi}} \rangle - \langle \underline {{\phi}}, \Pi_ {S} \underline {{\phi}} \rangle)
$$

by expanding the definition of  $\dot{\hat{\eta}}$  and  $\hat{\Lambda}$ . Therefore,  $\dot{\sigma} = 0$  implies that

$$
\langle \underline {{\phi}}, \underline {{\phi}} \rangle = \langle \underline {{\phi}}, \Pi_ {S} \underline {{\phi}} \rangle
$$

so that: after changing its values on a set  $U$  of measure zero under  $\mathcal{N}(\mu|_{\mathcal{M}}, \Sigma|_{\mathcal{M}})$  (and thus also under Lebesgue measure by Lemma G.6),  $\underline{\phi}$  is a linear combination of  $\{\hat{\phi}^i\}_{i=1}^r$ , i.e.

$$
\forall \vec {x} \notin U, \underline {{\phi}} (\vec {x}) = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (\vec {x})
$$

for some coefficients  $\{c_i\}_{i\in [r]}$ . By NullAvoid applied to  $A_{n\alpha} = U$  for all  $n$  and  $\alpha \in [n]$ , we also have that: a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\phi (g ^ {1}, \ldots , g ^ {\alpha}) = \underline {{\phi}} (\{g ^ {j} \} _ {j \in \mathcal {M}}) = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (\{g ^ {j} \} _ {j \in \mathcal {M}}) = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (g ^ {1}, \ldots , g ^ {\alpha}),
$$

and therefore, under the same condition, (recall  $A$  is the matrix giving rise to  $g$  in  $g\coloneqq Ah$ )

$$
g = A \phi (g ^ {1}, \dots , g ^ {\alpha}) = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} A \hat {\phi} ^ {i} (g ^ {1}, \dots , g ^ {\alpha}) = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} \hat {g} ^ {i}.
$$

This shows that, if we keep the core set as  $\mathcal{M}$ , then Basis is still satisfied. Since the core set is not changing, NullAvoid just follows from the induction hypothesis.

For usage later in the proof of Moments  $(m)$ , we record our observation here as follows

Lemma G.9. If  $\mathring{\sigma} = 0$ , then there are coefficients  $\{c_i\}_{i=1}^r$  independent of  $n$  such that a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
g = \sum_ {i \in [ r ]} c _ {i} \hat {g} ^ {i}.
$$

# G.4.2 If  $\dot{\sigma} > 0$ .

It's clear that  $g$  cannot be in the linear span of  $\{\hat{g}^i\}_{i \in [r]}$  asymptotically, so we will add  $g$  to the core set, and the Basis property follows immediately. In the below, we shall write  $\mathcal{M}$  for the old core set, and  $\mathcal{M}' \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathcal{M} \cup \{g\}$  for the new one.

It remains to show NullAvoid for  $\mathcal{M}'$ . First, let's assume that, a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,  $\Pi_{\tilde{H}}^{\perp}$  has no zero diagonal entry; we shall show this fact below in Lemma G.10. Because the conditional variance of  $g_{\alpha}^{m}$  given  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m - 1}$  is  $\sigma^2 (\Pi_{\tilde{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha}$ , and because  $\delta >0$ , this assumption implies that, a.s. for all large enough  $n$ ,

$$
g _ {\alpha} ^ {m} \left| g ^ {1}, \dots , g ^ {m - 1} \right. \text {h a s d e n s i t y f o r a l l} \alpha \in [ n ]. \tag {45}
$$

By "has density" here, we in particular mean that any Lesbegue measure zero set in  $\mathbb{R}$  has zero probability under the conditional distribution of  $g_{\alpha}^{m}$  given  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m - 1}$ .

Now, assuming Lemma G.10, we prove NullAvoid holds for  $\mathcal{M}'$ .

Let  $\{A_{n\alpha} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}'}\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}, \alpha \in [n]}$  be a triangular array of Lesbegue measure zero sets. For each  $A_{n\alpha}$ , define  $B_{n\alpha} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \{\vec{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}} : \lambda(A_{n\alpha}|_{\vec{x}}) \neq 0\}$ , where  $A_{n\alpha}|_{\vec{x}} = \{y \in \mathbb{R} : (\vec{x}, y) \in A_{n\alpha} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}} \times \mathbb{R}\}$  is the "slice" of  $A_{n\alpha}$  at  $\vec{x}$ , and  $\lambda$  is the 1-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Because each  $A_{n\alpha}$  has measure zero in  $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}'}$ , necessarily each  $B_{n\alpha}$  also has measure zero in  $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}$ . Applying NullAvoid to the triangular array  $\{B_{n\alpha} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}, \alpha \in [n]}$ , we get that: a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\forall \alpha \in [ n ], \left\{g _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \right\} _ {i \in \mathcal {M}} \notin B _ {n \alpha}.
$$

Therefore, by Eq. (45), a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\forall \alpha \in [ n ], \left\{g _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \right\} _ {i \in \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}} \notin A _ {n \alpha}.
$$

This finishes the proof of NullAvoid for  $\mathcal{M}'$ , and also  $\operatorname{CoreSet}(m)$ , save for Lemma G.10 below.

Lemma G.10. Almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,  $\Pi_H^\perp$  has no zero diagonal entry.

Proof. WLOG, assume  $\check{\Lambda}$  is full rank. Otherwise, by Lemma G.6(2), we can replace  $\check{h}^1,\ldots ,\check{h}^s$  by a linearly independent spanning set  $\check{h}^{i_1},\ldots ,\check{h}^{i_k}$  such that 1) each  $\check{h}^j$  is almost surely, for all large  $n$ , a linear combination of them and such that 2) their 2nd moment matrix is full rank in the limit. Then the projection matrix associated to  $\check{h}^{i_1},\ldots ,\check{h}^{i_k}$  is, almost surely, for all large  $n$ , the same as  $\Pi_{\check{H}}$ .

By the Sherman-Morrison formula (Fact G.11),

$$
\left(\Pi_ {\check {H}}\right) _ {\alpha \alpha} = f \left(\frac {1}{n} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {\top} \check {\Lambda} _ {- \alpha} ^ {- 1} \check {h} _ {\alpha}\right)
$$

where  $f(x) = x / (1 + x)$ ,  $\check{h}_{\alpha}$  is the column vector  $(\check{h}_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, \check{h}_{\alpha}^{s})^{\top}$ , and  $\check{\Lambda}_{-\alpha} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{\beta \neq \alpha} \check{h}_{\beta} \check{h}_{\beta}^{\top}$ . Thus, unless  $\check{\Lambda}_{-\alpha}$  is singular for some  $\alpha$ , all diagonal entries of  $\Pi_{\check{H}}^{\perp} = I - \Pi_{\check{H}}$  are nonzero. So it suffices to show that,

a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,  $\bar{\Lambda}_{-\alpha}$  is nonsingular for all  $\alpha$ .

To do this, it pays to note that  $\check{\Lambda}_{-\alpha} = \check{\Lambda} -\frac{1}{n}\check{h}_{\alpha}\check{h}_{\alpha}^{\top}$ , so that

$$
\left| \lambda_ {\min } \left(\check {\Lambda} _ {- \alpha}\right) - \lambda_ {\min } (\check {\Lambda}) \right| \leq \left\| \frac {1}{n} \check {h} _ {\alpha} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \frac {1}{n} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {\top} \check {h} _ {\alpha}.
$$

By Lemma G.12 below (which bounds the max by a high moment),

$$
\max  _ {\alpha \in n} \frac {1}{n} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {\top} \check {h} _ {\alpha} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0,
$$

and consequently

$$
\max  _ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \lambda_ {\min } (\breve {\Lambda} _ {- \alpha}) - \lambda_ {\min } (\breve {\Lambda}) | \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0.
$$

Because  $\check{\Lambda} \xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}} \check{\Lambda}$ , we know that, a.s. for large enough  $n$ ,  $\lambda_{\min}(\check{\Lambda})$  is bounded away from 0 by a constant (independent of  $n$ ). Altogether, this implies that all  $\check{\Lambda}_{-\alpha}$  are nonsingular, as desired.

Fact G.11 (Sherman-Morrison formula). For any nonsingular matrix  $A \in \mathbb{R}^{l \times l}$  and vector  $a \in \mathbb{R}^l$ , we have

$$
a ^ {\top} (A + a a ^ {\top}) ^ {- 1} a = \frac {a ^ {\top} A ^ {- 1} a}{1 + a ^ {\top} A ^ {- 1} a}.
$$

Consequently, for any full rank matrix  $H$ , the  $\alpha$ th diagonal entry of its associated projection matrix  $\Pi_H = H(H^\top H)^{-1}H^\top$  can be written as

$$
(\Pi_ {H}) _ {\alpha \alpha} = \frac {H _ {\alpha} (H _ {- \alpha} ^ {\top} H _ {- \alpha}) ^ {- 1} H _ {\alpha} ^ {\top}}{1 + H _ {\alpha} (H _ {- \alpha} ^ {\top} H _ {- \alpha}) ^ {- 1} H _ {\alpha} ^ {\top}}
$$

where  $H_{\alpha}$  is the  $\alpha$ th row of  $H$ , and  $H_{-\alpha}$  is  $H$  with the  $\alpha$ th row removed.

Lemma G.12. Assume Moments  $(m - 1)$ . Suppose  $\psi : \mathbb{R}^{m - 1} \to \mathbb{R}$  is polynomially bounded. Then as  $n \to \infty$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n ^ {p}} \max  _ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) | \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0
$$

for any  $p > 0$

Proof. For any  $q > 0$ , we have the elementary bound

$$
\max  _ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) | \leq \sqrt [ q ]{\sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} , \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) | ^ {q}}.
$$

Thus, for any  $q > 0$

$$
\frac {1}{n ^ {p}} \max  _ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) | \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {p - 1 / q}} \sqrt [ q ]{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} , \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}) | ^ {q}}.
$$

Because, by Moments  $(m - 1)$ ,  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha \in [n]}|\psi (g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1})|^{q}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}C$  for some constant  $C$  as  $n\to \infty$ , the RHS above converges a.s. to 0 as soon as we take  $q > 1 / p$ , and therefore so does the LHS.

![](images/907f011504168b1430a97c7092d0d8439f510437185769a58fc73ca682cd0a7c.jpg)

# G.5 Inductive Step: Moments(m)

In this section, we show

$$
\operatorname {M o m e n t s} (m - 1) \text {a n d} \operatorname {C o r e S e t} (m - 1) \Longrightarrow \operatorname {M o m e n t s} (m).
$$

More specifically, we will show that for any polynomially-bounded  $\psi : \mathbb{R}^m \to \mathbb{R}$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m}\right) \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z)
$$

where again on the RHS  $\psi$  ignores all coordinates  $Z^{m + 1},\ldots ,Z^M$  (corresponding to  $g^{m + 1},\ldots ,g^{M}$

By Lemma G.9, if  $\mathring{\sigma} = 0$ , then almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,  $g = g^{m}$  is just a (fixed) linear combination of  $g^{1}, \ldots, g^{m-1}$ , so Moments is trivially true. Therefore, in the below, we assume

$$
\stackrel {\circ} {\sigma} > 0. \quad (\star)
$$

This assumption will be crucial for our arguments involving smoothness induced by Gaussian averaging.

To clarify notation in the following, we will write  $\mathbb{E}_X$  [expression] to denote the expectation over only the randomization in  $X$ , and  $\mathbb{E}$  [expression|B] to denote the expectation taken over all randomness except those in  $\mathcal{B}$ .

Proof Plan Note that

$$
\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \psi (g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m}) - \underset {Z \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu , \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z) \right| \leq \mathsf {A} + \mathsf {B} + \mathsf {C}
$$

where

$\mathsf{A}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\psi (g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m}) - \mathbb{E}_{z}\psi \left(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1},\omega_{\alpha} + \sigma z\sqrt{(\Pi_{\check{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha\alpha}}\right)\right|$  
$\mathsf{B}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{z}\psi \left(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1},\omega_{\alpha} + \sigma z\sqrt{(\Pi_{\hat{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha\alpha}}\right) - \mathbb{E}_{z}\psi \left(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1},\sum_{i = 1}^{r}\hat{v}_{i}\hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i} + \bar{\sigma} z\right)\right|.$  
$\mathsf{C}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{z}\psi \left(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1},\sum_{i = 1}^{r}\bar{v}_{i}\hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i} + \mathring{\sigma} z\right) - \underset {Z\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu ,\Sigma)}{\mathbb{E}}\psi (Z)\right|$

with  $z \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$ . Note that  $\mathsf{B}$  and  $\mathsf{C}$  are random variables in  $\mathcal{B}$ . We will show that each of  $\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}$  goes to 0 almost surely, which would finish the proof of Theorem A.6.

Roughly speaking,  $\mathrm{A} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  because of a law of large number,  $\mathrm{B} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  because of the smoothness in  $\mathbb{E}_z \psi$  induced by Gaussian averaging, and  $\mathrm{C} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  by induction hypothesis. We start with the last item, since it's the easiest.

# G.5.1 C Converges Almost Surely to 0

In this section we show that  $C \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  by a straightforward reduction to the inductive hypothesis.

Let  $\hat{Z}^1, \ldots, \hat{Z}^r$  be the components of  $Z \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \Sigma)$  corresponding to  $\hat{g}^1, \ldots, \hat{g}^r$ , and let  $\hat{Z}$  be the column vector with these entries. Note that, by Proposition F.3,  $Z^m$  (corresponding to  $g^m$ ), conditioned on  $Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}$ , is distributed as a Gaussian with mean  $\Sigma(g, \hat{G})$ . Since  $\hat{G}, \hat{G}^+ \hat{Z} = \hat{\eta}^\top \hat{\Lambda}^+ \hat{Z} = \hat{v}^\top \hat{Z}$  and variance  $\Sigma(g, g) - \Sigma(g, \hat{G})\Sigma(\hat{G}, \hat{G})^+\Sigma(\hat{G}, g) = \hat{\sigma}$ . Thus

$$
\begin{array}{l} \underset {Z} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z) = \underset {Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1}} {\mathbb {E}} \mathbb {E} [ \psi (Z) | Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1} ] \\ = \underset {Z ^ {1},..., Z ^ {m - 1}} {\mathbb {E}} _ {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} \underset {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z ^ {1}, \ldots , Z ^ {m - 1}, \mathring {v} ^ {\top} \hat {Z} + \mathring {\sigma} z) \\ = \underset {Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1}} {\mathbb {E}} \Psi (Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1}) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have set  $\Psi(Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)} \psi(Z^1, \ldots, Z^{m-1}, \mathring{v}^\top \hat{Z} + \mathring{\sigma} z)$ .  $\Psi$  is a polynomially bounded function since  $\psi$  is. Applying the induction hypothesis to  $\Psi$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} _ {z} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} + \mathring {\sigma} z\right) \\ = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \Psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}\right) \\ \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} \underset {Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1}} {\mathbb {E}} \Psi (Z ^ {1}, \dots , Z ^ {m - 1}) \\ \end{array}
$$

by induction hypothesis

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \underset {Z ^ {1},..., Z ^ {m - 1}} {\mathbb {E}} \underset {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z ^ {1}, \ldots , Z ^ {m - 1}, \mathring {v} ^ {\top} \hat {Z} + \mathring {\sigma} z) \\ = \underset {Z} {\mathbb {E}} \psi (Z) \\ \end{array}
$$

as desired.

# G.5.2 A Converges Almost Surely to 0

In this section we show  $\mathrm{A}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}0$

For each  $\alpha \in [n]$ , let  $\psi_{\alpha}(x) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \psi(g_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, g_{\alpha}^{m-1}, \omega_{\alpha} + \sigma x)$ , with  $\omega$  and  $\sigma$  defined in Eq. (42). This is a random function depending on the randomness of  $g_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, g_{\alpha}^{m-1}$ , and it changes with  $n$  as well. Also consider the "centered version" of  $\psi_{\alpha}$ ,  $\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}(x) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \psi_{\alpha}(x) - \mathbb{E} \psi_{\alpha}(x')$  with expectation taken over  $x' \sim \mathcal{N}(0, (\Pi_{\tilde{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha})$  (but not  $g_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, g_{\alpha}^{m-1}$ ). Note by Eq. (43),

$$
\mathsf {A} \stackrel {\mathrm {d}} {=} _ {\mathcal {B}} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha})
$$

where  $\xi \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\Pi_H^\perp)$

Proof idea. To prove our claim, we will show that, for almost all (i.e. probability 1 in  $\mathcal{U}$ ) sequences of  $(g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m - 1}) = (g^{1}(n),\ldots ,g^{m - 1}(n))$  in  $n$  — which we shall call amenable sequences of  $g^1,\dots ,g^{m - 1}$  — we have a moment bound

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \mathsf {A} ^ {2 \lambda} | \mathcal {B} \right] = \underset {\xi \sim \mathcal {N} \left(0, \Pi_ {H} ^ {\perp}\right)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} \left(\xi_ {\alpha}\right)\right) ^ {2 \lambda} <   C n ^ {- 1. 2 5} \tag {46}
$$

for some large  $\lambda$  and some constant  $C > 0$  depending only on  $\lambda$  and the particular sequence of  $\{(g^{1}(n),\ldots ,g^{m - 1}(n))\}_{n}$ . Then we apply Lemma F.2 to show that, conditioned on any amenable sequence, A converges to 0 almost surely over all randomness remaining after conditioning. Since almost all sequences are amenable, this shows that the convergence is also almost sure without the conditioning.

The moment bound. For  $\lambda \geq 6$  and any  $q > 1$ , we first apply Theorem F.8 to get the bound

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\xi} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha})\right) ^ {2 \lambda} \leq c n ^ {- 1. 5 + 1 / q} \sqrt [ q ]{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha}) ^ {2 \lambda q}}
$$

where on both sides  $\xi \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\Pi_{\bar{H}}^{\perp})$ , and  $c$  is a constant depending only on  $\lambda$  and  $m$ , but not on  $n$ , the functions  $\psi_{\alpha}$ , or  $g^{1},\ldots ,g^{m - 1}$ . To obtain Eq. (46), we will show that

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} \left(\xi_ {\alpha}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q} \tag {47}
$$

is uniformly bounded (in  $n$ ), almost surely over the randomness of the sequences  $\{g^1(n), \ldots, g^{m-1}(n)\}_n$ . We take all such sequences to be the amenable sequences. For  $q > 4$ , we then get the desired moment bound Eq. (46).

It remains to show the almost sure uniform boundedness.

Almost sure uniform boundedness. Intuitively, Eq. (47) should converge almost surely to a deterministic value by applying some version of the induction hypothesis, so it should be almost surely uniformly bounded in  $n$ . The obstacle is that  $\xi_{\alpha}$  is not purely a function of  $g_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, g_{\alpha}^{m-1}$ , and a priori it is not clear how to apply the induction hypothesis in a straightforward way. We thus first process Eq. (47) a bit. Let  $\mu_{\alpha} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,(\Pi_{H}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha})} \psi_{\alpha}(x)$ . Then, abbreviating  $\mathbb{E}$  for expectation taken

over  $\xi \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\Pi_{\bar{H}}^{\perp})$ , we have the following inequalities of random variables in  $\mathcal{B}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha}) ^ {2 \lambda q} = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left(\psi_ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha}) - \mu_ {\alpha}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q} \\ \leq \frac {1}{n} 2 ^ {2 \lambda q - 1} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left[ \psi_ {\alpha} \left(\xi_ {\alpha}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q} + \mu_ {\alpha} ^ {2 \lambda q} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

by Lemma F.1

$$
\leq \frac {1}{n} 2 ^ {2 \lambda q} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \psi_ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha}) ^ {2 \lambda q}
$$

by power mean inequality  $\mu_{\alpha} \leq {}^{2\lambda q}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\psi_{\alpha}(\xi_{\alpha})^{2\lambda q}}$

$$
= \frac {1}{n} 2 ^ {2 \lambda q} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, \omega_ {\alpha} + \sigma \xi_ {\alpha}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q}.
$$

Suppose, WLOG, that  $\psi$  is polynomially bounded by an inequality  $|\psi(x)| \leq C \|x\|_p^p + c$  for some  $p, C, c > 0$ . In the below, we will silently introduce constants  $C_1, C_2, \ldots$  via Lemma F.1 and merge with old constants, such that they will only depend on  $\lambda, p, q$ . Continuing the chain of inequalities above

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \tilde {\psi} _ {\alpha} (\xi_ {\alpha}) ^ {2 \lambda q} \leq c + \frac {1}{n} C 2 ^ {2 \lambda q} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left(| g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | \omega_ {\alpha} + \sigma \xi_ {\alpha} | ^ {p}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q} \\ \leq c + \frac {1}{n} C _ {1} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left(| g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {p} + | \sigma \xi_ {\alpha} | ^ {p}\right) ^ {2 \lambda q} \\ \leq c + \frac {1}{n} C _ {2} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p} + \dots + \left| g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p} + \left| \omega_ {\alpha} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p} + \mathbb {E} \left| \sigma \xi_ {\alpha} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p}. \tag {48} \\ \end{array}
$$

We now proceed to show that the summands of Eq. (48) are almost surely uniformly bounded, which finishes our proof of  $\mathrm{A} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$ .

- By induction hypothesis,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left| g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p} + \dots + \left| g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} \right| ^ {2 \lambda q p}
$$

almost surely converges to a deterministic value, so that it is almost surely uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

- In addition,  $\sigma \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \mathring{\sigma}$ , so that, almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,  $\sigma \leq \mathring{\sigma} + 1$ . (The order of the qualifiers is important here; in general this statement cannot be made uniformly in  $n$ ). Therefore, almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} | \sigma \xi_ {\alpha} | ^ {2 \lambda q p} \leq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} | \stackrel {\circ} {\sigma} + 1 | ^ {2 \lambda q p} \mathbb {E} | \xi_ {\alpha} | ^ {2 \lambda q p}.
$$

This is almost surely uniformly bounded in  $n$  because  $\mathrm{Var}(\xi_{\alpha}) = (\Pi_{\bar{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha}\in [0,1]$  for all  $\alpha$  by Lemma F.7.

- It remains to bound  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha = 1}^{n}|\omega_{\alpha}|^{2\lambda qp}$ . We extract our reasoning here into the Lemma G.13 below, as we will need to reuse this for later. This finishes the proof of A a.s.  $\rightarrow$  0.

Lemma G.13. For any polynomially bounded function  $\varphi : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} | \varphi (\omega_ {\alpha}) |
$$

is almost surely uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

Proof. It suffices to prove this for  $\varphi (x) = |x|^d$  for any  $d > 0$ .

Expanding  $\omega$  according to Lemma G.5, we get

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {d} = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} (\mathring {v} _ {i} + \hat {\epsilon} _ {i}) + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| ^ {d}
$$

for (fixed dimensional)  $\hat{\epsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^r$ ,  $\check{\epsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^s$  that go to 0 almost surely with  $n$ . Applying Lemma F.1, we get

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {d} \leq \frac {1}{n} C _ {3} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \breve {v} _ {i} \right| ^ {d} + \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} \right| ^ {d} + \left| \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \breve {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \breve {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| ^ {d}.
$$

We bound each summand separately.

- By induction hypothesis,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \mathring {v} _ {i} \right| ^ {d}
$$

converges a.s. to a deterministic value, so it is a.s uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

- By the a.s. decaying property of  $\hat{\epsilon}$ , we have almost surely, for large enough  $n$ ,  $|\sum_{i=1}^{r} \hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i} \hat{\epsilon}_{i}| \leq \sum_{i=1}^{r} |\hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i}|$  (again, the order of qualifier is very important here). By induction hypothesis,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} | \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} |\right) ^ {d}
$$

converges a.s. to a deterministic value, yielding the a.s. uniformly-boundedness of it and of

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} \right| ^ {d}.
$$

- Likewise, because for each  $j$ ,  $\check{h}^j$  is a polynomially-bounded function of  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m - 1}$  the summands of

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left(\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} | \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} |\right) ^ {d}
$$

are polynomially-bounded functions of  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$  too. So by induction hypothesis, this sum converges a.s., implying the a.s. uniform boundedness of it and

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \left| \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| ^ {d}.
$$

![](images/1cee182b0874a6dbd3e4b3335aec80c07596fa0e578c228d416bcca3bf16395d.jpg)

# G.5.3 B Converges Almost Surely to 0

In this section we show  $\mathsf{B}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}0$

Some Notations For brevity, we will set  $d_{\alpha} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} (\Pi_{\bar{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha}$ . In addition, for each  $\alpha \in [n]$ ,  $w \in \mathbb{R}$ ,  $\tau \geq 0$ , let

$$
\Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \underset {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbb {E}} \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \ldots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, w + \tau z\right).
$$

(Here and in all that follows,  $\tau^2$  is the square of  $\tau$ , and the 2 is not an index). This is a random function, with randomness induced by  $g^1, \ldots, g^{m-1}$ .

Our proof idea is to write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathsf {B} = \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {n} \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in U} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) \right| + \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| (49) \\ + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| (50) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $U \sqcup V = [n]$  is a partition of  $[n]$  with  $U \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \{ \alpha : d_{\alpha} < 1/2 \}$  and  $V$  is its complement. Note that  $|U| \leq 2$  rank  $\check{H} \leq 2s$  is uniformly bounded in  $n$ . We then show each summand of Eq. (49) goes to 0 a.s. independently. Finally we use the smoothness of  $\Psi_{\alpha}$  (Eq. (52)) induced by the Gaussian averaging in  $\Psi_{\alpha}$  to show each summand of Eq. (50) is almost surely  $o(1/n)$ , finishing the proof.

Eq. (49) converges to 0 a.s. We first look at the term

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in U} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| \leq \frac {| U |}{n} \max  _ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq \frac {2 s}{n ^ {1 - 1 / q}} \sqrt [ q ]{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| ^ {q}} \tag {51} \\ \end{array}
$$

for any  $q > 0$ . Now  $\left|\Psi_{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i = 1}^{r}\mathring{v}_{i}\hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i};\hat{\sigma}^{2}\right)\right|^{q}$  is a fixed (independent of  $\alpha$ ) polynomially-bounded function of  $g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots ,g_{\alpha}^{m - 1}$ , so by induction hypothesis,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \mathring {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| ^ {q}
$$

is a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ , so that using a large  $q \geq 2$ , we see Eq. (51) converges a.s. to 0.

Next, we apply a similar reasoning to the other term and obtain

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in U} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) \right| \leq \frac {2 s}{n ^ {1 - 1 / q}} \sqrt [ q ]{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) \right| ^ {q}}
$$

We in fact already know that

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) \right| ^ {q}
$$

is a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$  from Eq. (48) in Appendix G.5.2, so that

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in U} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) \right| \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0
$$

from which follows the same for Eq. (49).

Eq. (50) converges to 0 a.s. As mentioned above, to prove this we will use the following smoothness bound of  $\Psi_{\alpha}$ , whose proof will be delayed to the end of the section. Suppose, WLOG, that the polynomially boundedness of  $\psi$  presents itself in an inequality  $|\psi(x)| \leq C \|x\|_p^p + C$ , for some  $p, C > 0$ , where  $p$  is an integer. This  $p$  will appear explicitly in this smoothness bound below.

Lemma G.14 (Smoothness of  $\Psi_{\alpha}$ ). Let  $w, \Delta w \in \mathbb{R}, \tau^2, \Delta \tau^2 \in \mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}$ . Then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} (w + \Delta w; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2}) - \Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) \right| \\ \leq R \left(| \Delta w | + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) \left(1 + \tau^ {- 2}\right) \left(S _ {\alpha} + | w | ^ {p} + | \Delta w | ^ {p} + \tau^ {p} + \left(\Delta \tau^ {2}\right) ^ {p / 2}\right) \tag {52} \\ \end{array}
$$

for some constant  $R > 0$ , and where

$$
S _ {\alpha} \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} 1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p}.
$$

To bound Eq. (50), first we expand

$$
\omega_ {\alpha} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} (\mathring {v} _ {i} + \hat {\epsilon} _ {i}) + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j}
$$

where, by Lemma G.5,  $\hat{\epsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^r$ ,  $\check{\epsilon} \in \mathbb{R}^s$  are vectors that go to 0 almost surely with  $n$ . Then we apply the smoothness bound Eq. (52) to get, for each  $\alpha \in V$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| \leq R \left(1 + \min  \left(\sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) X _ {\alpha} Y _ {\alpha} \\ \leq R \left(1 + \min  \left(\sigma^ {2} / 2, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) X _ {\alpha} Y _ {\alpha} \\ \end{array}
$$

using the fact that  $d_{\alpha} \geq 1/2, \forall \alpha \in V$ . Here

$$
\begin{array}{l} X _ {\alpha} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \left| \omega_ {\alpha} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \right| + \left| \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} - \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2} \right| \\ = \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| + | \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} - \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2} | \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
Y _ {\alpha} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} S _ {\alpha} + | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {p} + \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| ^ {p} + \max  (\sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}, \mathfrak {s h} ^ {2}) ^ {p / 2} + | \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} - \mathfrak {s h} ^ {2} | ^ {p / 2}.
$$

Thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} E q. (5 0) = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\omega_ {\alpha}; \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \mathring {v} _ {i} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}; \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq R \frac {1}{n} \left(1 + \min  \left(\sigma^ {2} / 2, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) \sum_ {\alpha \in V} X _ {\alpha} Y _ {\alpha} \\ \leq R \left(1 + \min  \left(\sigma^ {2} / 2, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} X _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} Y _ {\alpha} ^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Since  $\sigma \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} \mathring{\sigma}$  and we have assumed  $\mathring{\sigma} > 0$  by Eq. ( $\star$ ), we have  $\left(1 + \min \left(\sigma^2 / 2, \mathring{\sigma}^2\right)^{-1}\right)$  is almost surely uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

Thus, Eq. (50) can be shown to converge a.s. to 0 if we show

$\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha \in V}Y_{\alpha}^{2}}$  is a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ , and

$$
\sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} X _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0
$$

We prove these two claims in Lemmas G.15 and G.16 below, which would finish our proof of  $\mathsf{B}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}0$ , and of our main theorem Theorem A.6 as well.

Lemma G.15.  $\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha\in V}X_{\alpha}^{2}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}0.$

Proof. Note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} X _ {\alpha} \leq \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| + | \check {\sigma} ^ {2} - \sigma^ {2} | + | \sigma^ {2} - \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} | \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} P _ {\alpha} + Q _ {\alpha} + R _ {\alpha}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then by triangle inequality (in  $\ell_2$ -norm),

$$
\sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} X _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} P _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} Q _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} R _ {\alpha} ^ {2}}.
$$

We now show that each term above converges a.s. to 0, which would finish the proof of Lemma G.15.

- Because  $\hat{\epsilon} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$  and  $\check{\epsilon} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}} 0$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} P _ {\alpha} ^ {2} \leq C _ {8} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \left(\hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i}\right) ^ {2} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \left(\check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq C _ {8} \max  _ {i, j} \left\{\left| \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} \right|, \left| \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| \right\} \times \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \left(\hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}\right) ^ {2} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \left(\check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq C _ {8} \max  _ {i, j} \left\{\left| \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} \right|, \left| \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| \right\} \times \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \left(\hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i}\right) ^ {2} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \left(\check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} C _ {8} \times 0 \times \mathcal {E} = 0 \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathcal{E}$  is the Gaussian expectation that  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha \in [n]}\left(\sum_{i = 1}^{r}(\hat{g}_{\alpha}^{i})^{2} + \sum_{j = 1}^{s}(\check{h}_{\alpha}^{j})^{2}\right)$  converges a.s. to, by inductive hypothesis.

- The quantity  $Q_{\alpha}$  actually doesn't depend on  $\alpha$ , so that

$$
\sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} Q _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} \leq | \dot {\sigma} ^ {2} - \sigma^ {2} | \xrightarrow {\mathrm {a . s .}} 0
$$

by Lemma G.4.

- Notice  $R_{\alpha}^{2} = \sigma^{4}(1 - d_{\alpha})^{2} \leq \sigma^{4}(1 - d_{\alpha})$  because  $1 - d_{\alpha} \in [0,1/2]$ . Thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} R _ {\alpha} ^ {2} \leq \sigma^ {4} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} 1 - d _ {\alpha} \\ \leq \sigma^ {4} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} 1 - d _ {\alpha} \\ = \sigma^ {4} \frac {1}{n} \operatorname {r a n k} \check {H} \\ \end{array}
$$

by the definition that  $d_{\alpha} = (\Pi_{\tilde{H}}^{\perp})_{\alpha \alpha}$ . But of course  $\text{rank } \tilde{H} \leq s$  is bounded relative to  $n$ . So this quantity goes to 0 (surely) as desired.

![](images/8902c772098dfaf037ecd5a00c52a783b03a300cf9afc3b1c782e295c46aa2f8.jpg)

Lemma G.16.  $\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha \in V}Y_{\alpha}^{2}}$  is a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

Proof. We have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} Y _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} S _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {2 p}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} X _ {\alpha} ^ {\prime 2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in V} \max (\sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} , \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}) ^ {p}} \\ \leq \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} S _ {\alpha} ^ {2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {2 p}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} X _ {\alpha} ^ {\prime 2}} + \sqrt {\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \max (\sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} , \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}) ^ {p}} \\ \end{array}
$$

where

$$
X _ {\alpha} ^ {\prime} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \hat {g} _ {\alpha} ^ {i} \hat {\epsilon} _ {i} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \check {h} _ {\alpha} ^ {j} \check {\epsilon} _ {j} \right| ^ {p} + | \sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha} - \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2} | ^ {p}
$$

We proceed to show that each of 4 summands above are individually a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

-  $S_{\alpha}^{2}$  is a polynomially bounded function of  $g_{\alpha}^{1}, \ldots, g_{\alpha}^{m - 1}$ , so that by Moments(  $m - 1$ ),

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} S _ {\alpha} ^ {2} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} C
$$

for some constant  $C$ , so it is also a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

- By Lemma G.13, we get

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} | \omega_ {\alpha} | ^ {2 p}
$$

is a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

- Using the same reasoning as in the proof of Lemma G.15, one can easily show

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} X _ {\alpha} ^ {\prime 2} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} 0
$$

so it is also a.s. uniformly bounded.

- Since  $d_{\alpha} \leq 1$ , we have  $\max(\sigma^2 d_{\alpha}, \mathring{\sigma}^2) \leq \max(\sigma^2, \mathring{\sigma}^2)$ , which is independent of  $\alpha$ . Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \max  \left(\sigma^ {2} d _ {\alpha}, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {p} \leq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\alpha \in [ n ]} \max  \left(\sigma^ {2}, \mathring {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {p} \\ = \max  \left(\sigma^ {2}, \dot {\sigma} ^ {2}\right) ^ {p / 2} \xrightarrow {\text {a . s .}} \hat {\sigma} ^ {p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, it is also a.s. uniformly bounded in  $n$ .

![](images/71395ed133f5c466bc75b8e1a0875e2a2f492968486614dcecd424aec148ad49.jpg)

Finally, we deliver the promised proof of Lemma G.14.

Proof of Lemma G.14. By Lemma F.4,  $\Psi_{\alpha}$  is differentiable in  $w$ , and

$$
\partial_ {w} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) = \tau^ {- 1} \underset {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbb {E}} z \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, w + \tau z\right) \tag {53}
$$

$$
\partial_ {\tau^ {2}} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) = \frac {1}{2} \tau^ {- 2} \underset {z \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(z ^ {2} - 1\right) \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, w + \tau z\right). \tag {54}
$$

Recall that  $|\psi(x)| \leq C \| x \|_p^p + C$ . We will silently introduce constants  $C_1, C_2, \ldots$  depending only on  $p$ , merging with old constants, typically via Lemma F.1 or by integrating out some integrands

depending only on  $p$ . With  $z \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \partial_ {w} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) \right| \leq \tau^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} _ {z} | z | | \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, w + \tau z\right) | \\ \leq \tau^ {- 1} C _ {z} \mathbb {E} | z | \left(1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | w + \tau z | ^ {p}\right) \\ \leq \tau^ {- 1} C _ {1} \mathbb {E} _ {z} | z | \left(1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | w | ^ {p} + \tau^ {p} | z | ^ {p}\right) \\ \leq \tau^ {- 1} C _ {2} \left(1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | w | ^ {p} + \tau^ {p}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Similarly,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \partial_ {\tau^ {2}} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w; \tau^ {2}) \right| \leq \frac {1}{2} \tau^ {- 2} \mathbb {E} _ {z} | z ^ {2} - 1 | | \psi \left(g _ {\alpha} ^ {1}, \dots , g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1}, w + \tau z\right) | \\ \leq \tau^ {- 2} C _ {3} \left(1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p} + | w | ^ {p} + \tau^ {p}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, for any  $\Delta w\in \mathbb{R}$ $\Delta \tau^2\in \mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}$  , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(w + \Delta w; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(w; \tau^ {2}\right) \right| \\ = \left| \int_ {0} ^ {1} d t \left(\Delta w \cdot \partial_ {w} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w + \Delta w t; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t) + \Delta \tau^ {2} \cdot \partial_ {\tau^ {2}} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w + \Delta w t; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t)\right) \right| \\ \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} d t \left(| \Delta w | \cdot | \partial_ {w} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w + \Delta w t; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t) | + | \Delta \tau^ {2} | \cdot | \partial_ {\tau^ {2}} \Psi_ {\alpha} (w + \Delta w t; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t) |\right) \\ \leq \left(C _ {2} + C _ {3}\right) \left(\left| \Delta w \right| + \left| \Delta \tau^ {2} \right|\right) \\ \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mathrm {d} t \left(\left(\tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} + \left(\tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t\right) ^ {- 1}\right) \times \left(S _ {\alpha} + | w + \Delta w t | ^ {p} + \left(\tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t\right) ^ {p / 2}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where for brevity we have set

$$
S _ {\alpha} \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} 1 + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {1} | ^ {p} + \dots + | g _ {\alpha} ^ {m - 1} | ^ {p},
$$

which is independent of  $t$ .

Since  $\Delta \tau^2 \geq 0$ ,  $(\tau^2 + \Delta \tau^2 t)^{-1} \leq \tau^{-2}$ , and we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(w + \Delta w; \tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) - \Psi_ {\alpha} \left(w; \tau^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq C _ {4} \left(\left| \Delta w \right| + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) \left(\tau^ {- 1} + \tau^ {- 2}\right) \int_ {0} ^ {1} d t \left(S _ {\alpha} + \left| w + \Delta w t \right| ^ {p} + \left(\tau^ {2} + \Delta \tau^ {2} t\right) ^ {p / 2}\right) \\ \leq C _ {5} \left(| \Delta w | + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) \left(\tau^ {- 1} + \tau^ {- 2}\right) \int_ {0} ^ {1} d t \left(S _ {\alpha} + | w | ^ {p} + | \Delta w | ^ {p} t ^ {p} + \tau^ {p} + (\Delta \tau^ {2}) ^ {p / 2} t ^ {p / 2}\right) \\ \leq C _ {6} \left(\left| \Delta w \right| + \Delta \tau^ {2}\right) \left(\tau^ {- 1} + \tau^ {- 2}\right) \left(S _ {\alpha} + \left| w \right| ^ {p} + \left| \Delta w \right| ^ {p} + \tau^ {p} + \left(\Delta \tau^ {2}\right) ^ {p / 2}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the end we have integrated out  $t^p$  and  $t^{p / 2}$ . We finally apply the simplification  $\tau^{-1} \leq \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\tau^{-2}$  by AM-GM to get the desired Eq. (52).

![](images/b0ebd5fdf0f5ce8275aa3e8daa89070273c848d9e7bd87875e76710df2083aef.jpg)
